# The Essential Collection of the Complete Library in Four Sections <note>original: 四庫全書薈要, Siku Quanshu Huiyao</note> from the Hall of Displaying Elegant Prose <note>original: 摛藻堂, Chizao Tang; this was the specific library hall within the Forbidden City where the Qianlong Emperor kept this condensed version of his massive literary compendium</note>

### The Masters Section <note>original: 子部, Zibu; the third of the four traditional divisions of Chinese literature, which includes works on philosophy, religion, arts, and sciences</note>

</insert>

<summary>This is a formal title label for a volume within the imperial "Essential Collection of the Complete Library in Four Sections," marking it as part of the philosophical and technical "Masters" category. It identifies the book as belonging to the Chizao Tang, a specialized library within the Forbidden City.</summary>

# Imperially Commissioned Essential Collection of the Complete Library in Four Sections <note>original: 欽定四庫全書薈要, *Qinding Siku Quanshu Huiyao*. This was a condensed version of the largest collection of books in Chinese history, created specifically for the Emperor's personal use.</note>

### Masters Division <note>original: 子部, *Zibu*. The third of the four traditional branches of Chinese knowledge, encompassing philosophy, religion, and the arts and sciences—including mathematics.</note>

### Imperially Authored Mathematical Essentials: Tables, Volume 4 of 5 <note>original: 御製數理精蘊表, *Yuzhi Shuli Jingyun Biao*. This work was a definitive encyclopedia of mathematics compiled during the Kangxi reign, combining traditional Chinese methods with European mathematical knowledge brought by Jesuit missionaries.</note>
</insert>

Detailed Proofreading Official and Secretary, your servant Chen Mu <note>original: 詳校官主事臣陳木. Chen Mu was the official responsible for ensuring the accuracy of this specific transcription. The term 'servant' (*chen*) was a standard way for officials to refer to themselves when submitting work to the Emperor.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the formal opening for a specific section within the mathematical tables of the "Complete Library in Four Sections" (Siku Quanshu Huiyao). # Imperially Authored Mathematical Essentials: Tables <note>original: 御製數理精蘊表, *Yuzhi Shuli Jingyun Biao*. Compiled during the reign of the Kangxi Emperor (r. 1661–1722), this work was a definitive encyclopedia that integrated traditional Chinese mathematical knowledge with European methods brought by Jesuit missionaries.</note>

### Imperially Commissioned Essential Collection of the Complete Library in Four Sections, Volume 10,877 <gloss>Masters Division</gloss> <note>original: 欽定四庫全書薈要, *Qinding Siku Quanshu Huiyao*. This was a more exclusive, condensed version of the larger Siku Quanshu, intended for the Emperor's personal use. The "Masters Division" (Zibu) categorized works on philosophy, science, and the arts.</note>

### Imperially Authored Mathematical Essentials: Tables, Volume 4 of 5

### Detailed Clarification of Logarithms, Part 10 <note>original: 對數闡微, *Duishu Chanwei*. The term 'duishu' (paired numbers) was the early Chinese translation for logarithms. This section specifically addresses the 'subtle details' or 'clarification' of logarithmic principles and their practical application in tables.</note>

---

-><note>Collector's Seal: Xiao Family of Weiyang (original: 維揚蕭氏). This seal, found at the top of the page, indicates that this particular manuscript was once part of the private library of the Xiao family from Yangzhou (historically known as Weiyang), a city famous for its wealthy merchant-scholars.</note><-

<summary>This page functions as a formal title header for the tenth section of logarithmic tables within the "Mathematical Essentials." It situates the work within the elite "Essential Collection" of the Emperor's private library, reflecting the high value placed on mathematical precision in the Qing court.</summary>

<keywords>Logarithms, Siku Quanshu Huiyao, Mathematical Essentials, Qing Dynasty, Imperial Library, Xiao Family, mathematical tables</keywords>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library in Four Sections <note>original: 欽定四庫全書, *Qinding Siku Quanshu*. This monumental project was the largest collection of books in Chinese history, compiled by order of the Qianlong Emperor in the 18th century to preserve and categorize all known Chinese knowledge into four branches: Classics, History, Philosophy (which included science and mathematics), and Belles-Lettres.</note>

### Volume 4 of 5

<vocab><term>Imperially Commissioned Complete Library in Four Sections</term>, <term>Volume</term></vocab>

<summary>This page serves as a formal header and volume marker for the "Mathematical Essentials" within the Imperial Library collection. It identifies the work as belonging to the fourth of five volumes in this specific mathematical sub-series.</summary>

<keywords>Complete Library in Four Sections, Siku Quanshu, Volume 4, imperial collection, mathematical archives</keywords>

Small characters in original glosses were occasionally blurred; these numbers reflect the clearest possible reading from the transcription.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

<margin>Volume 4, Part 5</margin>

<note>This table presents a series of mathematical values. # Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 4, Section 5

<note>This page consists of a mathematical table, likely logarithms, which were a centerpiece of the "Shuli Jingyun," an encyclopedic work on mathematics compiled under the direction of the Kangxi Emperor. The large numbers represent the main entries, while the small interlinear numbers (glosses) are used for interpolation—calculating more precise values between the main entries.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Harmony

<note>The following table is a high-precision calculation tool from the Qing Dynasty. The large numbers are the primary entries (likely part of a logarithmic or trigonometric table), while the smaller numbers—represented here in <gloss>tags</gloss>—are "proportional parts." These allowed mathematicians of the imperial court to calculate values that fell between the main entries, providing accuracy essential for tracking celestial bodies or surveying land.</note>

The numbers here represent part of a table of common logarithms (base 10). <note>This table provides the numerical components for advanced calculations. The large numbers in each cell are the "index" or "argument," while the smaller numbers (glosses) represent the fractional parts and the "common difference"—the amount added to get to the next value in the series. Think of this as a 1700s version of a scientific calculator's memory.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Collected Essence of Mathematical Principles

<note>The following tables are a historical equivalent to a modern scientific calculator's reference data. Each large five-digit number is the "argument" (the number being calculated), and the smaller numbers below it in the <gloss> tags represent the fractional parts of its logarithm (the mantissa) and the difference between consecutive values, used for high-precision astronomy and engineering during the Qing dynasty.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

->Volume 4, Part 5<-

<note>This page contains a highly detailed numerical table used for logarithmic calculations. In the Qing dynasty, such tables were essential for astronomers and mathematicians to perform complex multiplications and divisions by adding or subtracting the "glossed" values (logarithms). The large five-digit numbers are the base values (arguments), while the smaller numbers below them represent the mantissa (the fractional part of the logarithm). To save space and improve readability for the period's scholars, the leading digits of the logarithms—which remain the same over a range of numbers—are often omitted or noted only when they change.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Principles

<margin>Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Principles</margin>
<margin>13</margin>

<note>This table is part of the "Essence of Mathematical Principles" (*Shuli Jingyun*), a monumental mathematical compendium commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor in the early 18th century. The numbers listed are arguments for common logarithms. To find the logarithm of a number, a scholar would locate the value in this grid. The small interlinear glosses are "proportional parts" or differences used for interpolation—calculating values between the whole numbers listed. This reflects the blend of traditional Chinese calculation and Western mathematical techniques brought by Jesuit scholars to the Qing court.</note>

<note>This table shows a high-precision numerical sequence where each entry includes a large six-digit number and a smaller interlinear "gloss" number. In the mathematical context of the Qing dynasty, these glosses often represented "differences" (*cha*) used for interpolation—allowing the user to calculate values between the main table entries. The columns are organized by the Celestial Stems, a traditional Chinese counting system, showing the synthesis of traditional Chinese bureaucracy and Western-influenced mathematics.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 145
<note>The Siku Quanshu, or "Complete Library of the Four Treasuries," was a massive collection of Chinese knowledge compiled in the 18th century. Volume 145 contains the "Shuli Jingyun" (Essence of Mathematical Principles), which integrated Western mathematical techniques brought by Jesuits with traditional Chinese mathematics.</note>

<note>These tables are part of the Shuli Jingyun (1723), a monumental mathematical compendium compiled under the direction of the Kangxi Emperor. The numbers likely represent common logarithms (base 10). The large five-digit number is the index, while the smaller interlinear numbers (glosses) represent the fractional part (mantissa) and the difference for interpolation.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume 4, Part 5

<note>This volume contains tables of logarithms, a revolutionary mathematical tool introduced to the Qing court by Jesuit scholars and further refined by Chinese mathematicians under the Kangxi Emperor's direction. The large five-digit numbers at the top of each cell are the "argument" or index, while the numbers in the glosses represent the fractional part of the logarithm (the mantissa) and the difference for calculation.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Imperially Produced Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Principles
### Page 18

<note>This table presents common logarithms (base 10). The five-digit number in each cell is the "argument" or "index" (the number being logged). The six-digit sequence in the <gloss> tags represents the "mantissa" (the decimal part of the logarithm). In 18th-century mathematics, these tables were essential for performing complex multiplication and division by converting them into addition and subtraction.</note>

<note>The main numbers in the table represent logarithmic indices. The four-digit interlinear glosses (e.g., 1983, 1982) are the terminal digits of the logarithm, while the bottom two-digit numbers (31) represent the constant difference between consecutive entries, used for linear interpolation to find values between the listed indices.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Collected Essence of Mathematical Principles

<note>These tables function as a mathematical reference for logarithms. The large numbers in the main cells are the index numbers (the numbers to be looked up), while the small interlinear glosses represent the mantissa—the decimal part of the logarithm—or the proportional differences used to calculate precise values. The "Imperially Commissioned" status reflects the Kangxi Emperor's personal interest in bringing Western mathematical precision to the Qing court.</note>

# Imperially Produced Table of the Essence of Mathematical Science
<note>The Shuli Jingyun (1723) is a massive mathematical compendium commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor. These tables represent the peak of Qing dynasty mathematics, combining traditional Chinese calculation methods with Western logarithmic and trigonometric theories brought by Jesuit scholars.</note>

# Imperially Produced Table of the Essence of Mathematical Science
## Volume 4, Part 5

<note>This table is part of the Shuli Jingyun (1723), an encyclopedic work on mathematics and astronomy commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor. It contains a table of common logarithms (base 10). The large numbers are the "indices" (the numbers being looked up), and the small interlinear text (glosses) contains the "mantissa" or the decimal portion of the logarithm, calculated to a high degree of precision for 18th-century science.</note>

# Imperially Produced Table of the Essence of Mathematical Science

<note>The *Shuli Jingyun* (1723), or "Essence of Mathematical Science," was an expansive mathematical encyclopedia commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor. This specific table provides common logarithms (base 10). The large digits in each cell represent the number being looked up (the argument), while the smaller interlinear text—the glosses—contains the "mantissa" or the decimal portion of the logarithm, calculated to several decimal places. In the 18th century, these tables were revolutionary for simplifying complex astronomical and engineering calculations by allowing multiplication to be performed as addition.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

<margin>
Imperially
Commissioned
Complete
Library
of the
Four 
Treasuries

Volume 4, 
Part 5
</margin>

<note>This table is a section of a "Table of Logarithms." In the 18th century, these tables were the calculators of the age. By looking up a number (the large digits), a mathematician or astronomer could find its corresponding logarithm (the small interlinear numbers, or "glosses"). This transformed difficult multiplication problems into simple addition. The small numbers in the glosses represent the fractional part of the logarithm, known as the <term>mantissa</term>—the decimal part following the whole number.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Science

<margin>Imperially Commissioned Tables of the Essence of Mathematical Science</margin>

<note>This table is part of the *Shuli Jingyun* (Essence of Mathematical Science), a massive compendium of mathematics commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor and published in 1723. These specific pages contain tables of <term>mantissas</term> (the decimal part of a logarithm). To use this, a scholar would find their number (the large five-digit figures) and then use the interlinear small numbers to determine the precise logarithmic value, used for complex astronomical or engineering calculations.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume 4, Part 5

<note>This volume belongs to the *Shuli Jingyun* (Essence of Mathematical Science), the definitive mathematical encyclopedia of the Qing Dynasty. Commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor, it integrated Western mathematical knowledge—brought by Jesuit missionaries—with traditional Chinese methods. The table below represents a sequence of <term>logarithmic mantissas</term>. Each large number is the argument, and the smaller numbers in the interlinear annotations represent the fractional values needed to determine precise logarithms.</note>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume 4 of 5

<note>This table is a traditional logarithmic table. The bold five-digit numbers represent the "argument" (the number whose logarithm is being sought). The interlinear glosses in smaller text provide the fractional part of the logarithm, used to facilitate complex calculations like multiplication through simple addition. In the original format, these columns were read from right to left, but they are presented here following the layout of the transcription.</note>

<note>Historical Context: These tables contain common logarithms. The bold main entries (99101, etc.) represent the sequence of numbers being calculated. The interlinear glosses in smaller script—such as 8860 and 14—are the specific mantissas or differences between values. In an era before mechanical calculators, these tables were essential for astronomers to convert long multiplication into simple addition, or division into subtraction.</note>

<summary>This page contains a detailed mathematical table of logarithmic values for numbers ranging from 99861 to 99120. It forms part of the Shuli Jingyun, a definitive 18th-century compilation of imperial Chinese and European mathematics.</summary>

These tables were essential tools for imperial astronomers and mathematicians for performing complex multiplications and divisions through addition and subtraction.</meta>

# Metal Geng, Division of the Fourth Month
<note>Metal Geng (Jin Geng) refers to the seventh Heavenly Stem, "Geng," which is associated with the element of Metal. In this context, it serves as a header for a specific section of the astronomical or mathematical ephemeris.</note>
